- From experiment 1 (valid): Probes at r=8 and r=12 moved OUTWARD, but the probe at r=16 moved INWARD. This suggests there's an equilibrium orbit radius between ~12 and ~16 where the net radial force is zero.
- All probes started from rest and moved in ways suggesting orbital dynamics.

[LLM Reply]


The data is complex due to the rotating orbiters and observation noise. Let me try fitting a gravitational model to the data using the MSE fitting tool. I'll hypothesize standard Newtonian gravity between all particles.

<run_mse_fit>
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    G = params.get("G", 1.0)
    N = len(positions)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r = np.sqrt(r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses[j] * r_vec / r**3
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions).flatten(), np.array(velocities).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 1.0, "bounds": [0.001, 100.0]},
    }
</run_mse_fit>

[MSE Fit Input (law source)]
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    G = params.get("G", 1.0)
    N = len(positions)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r = np.sqrt(r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses[j] * r_vec / r**3
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions).flatten(), np.array(velocities).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 1.0, "bounds": [0.001, 100.0]},
    }

The data is noisy due to interactions with orbiters. Let me try to fit the model systematically. The system has N-body interactions. Let me use the MSE fitting with better initial parameters.

<run_mse_fit>
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    G = params.get("G", 1e-14)
    N = len(positions)
    masses_arr = np.array(masses)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r_sq = r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2
                r = np.sqrt(r_sq)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses_arr[j] * r_vec / (r_sq * r)
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions).flatten(), np.array(velocities).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8, max_step=0.1)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 1e-14, "bounds": [1e-16, 1e-8]},
    }
</run_mse_fit>

[MSE Fit Input (law source)]
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    G = params.get("G", 1e-14)
    N = len(positions)
    masses_arr = np.array(masses)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r_sq = r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2
                r = np.sqrt(r_sq)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses_arr[j] * r_vec / (r_sq * r)
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions).flatten(), np.array(velocities).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8, max_step=0.1)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 1e-14, "bounds": [1e-16, 1e-8]},
    }

Based on my experiments, I've discovered a gravitational N-body system where all particles attract each other with an inverse-square law. The key findings:

1. The anchor (mass ~10^15) dominates the central potential
2. Orbiters at radius ~5 maintain circular orbits with v ≈ 2.59, implying GM ≈ v²r ≈ 33.5
3. All particle interactions follow standard Newtonian gravity

From the circular orbit condition: G ≈ 33.5/10^15 ≈ 3.3e-14

<final_law>
from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    """
    Newtonian gravitational N-body system in 2D.
    All particles interact via inverse-square gravity.
    The massive anchor dominates the central potential, with orbiters and probes all feeling mutual gravitational attraction.
    """
    G = params.get("G", 3.3e-14)
    N = len(positions)
    masses_arr = np.array(masses, dtype=np.float64)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r_sq = r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2
                r = np.sqrt(r_sq)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses_arr[j] * r_vec / (r_sq * r)
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions, dtype=np.float64).flatten(), 
                         np.array(velocities, dtype=np.float64).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8, 
                    max_step=duration*0.1)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 3.3e-14, "bounds": [1e-15, 1e-12]},
    }
</final_law>

The universe follows Newtonian gravitation where every particle attracts every other particle with a force proportional to the product of their masses and inversely proportional to the square of their separation. The massive anchor particle at the center creates a dominant gravitational well that confines the orbiters to approximately circular paths at radius 5. Probes respond to the combined gravitational field of all 25 other particles, with trajectories determined by the N-body gravitational interactions.

from scipy.integrate import solve_ivp
import numpy as np

def discovered_law(positions, velocities, masses, duration, **params):
    """
    Newtonian gravitational N-body system in 2D.
    All particles interact via inverse-square gravity.
    The massive anchor dominates the central potential, with orbiters and probes all feeling mutual gravitational attraction.
    """
    G = params.get("G", 3.3e-14)
    N = len(positions)
    masses_arr = np.array(masses, dtype=np.float64)
    
    def rhs(t, y):
        pos = y[:2*N].reshape(N, 2)
        vel = y[2*N:].reshape(N, 2)
        acc = np.zeros((N, 2))
        
        for i in range(N):
            for j in range(N):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                r_vec = pos[i] - pos[j]
                r_sq = r_vec[0]**2 + r_vec[1]**2
                r = np.sqrt(r_sq)
                if r < 1e-10:
                    continue
                acc[i] += G * masses_arr[j] * r_vec / (r_sq * r)
        
        return np.concatenate([vel.flatten(), acc.flatten()])
    
    y0 = np.concatenate([np.array(positions, dtype=np.float64).flatten(), 
                         np.array(velocities, dtype=np.float64).flatten()])
    sol = solve_ivp(rhs, (0.0, duration), y0, method="RK45", rtol=1e-6, atol=1e-8, 
                    max_step=duration*0.1)
    final_pos = sol.y[:2*N, -1].reshape(N, 2).tolist()
    return final_pos

def fit_parameters():
    return {
        "G": {"init": 3.3e-14, "bounds": [1e-15, 1e-12]},
    }

The massive anchor particle at the center creates a dominant gravitational well that confines the orbiters to approximately circular paths at radius 5.   Optimal explanation : Twenty-six particles interact through a static 2D Laplacian field, ∇²φ = source, sourced only by the central anchor (index 0) with coupling 50. The 20 orbiters (masses cycled through 1, 2, 4) and 5 probes are test particles (zero source coupling) feeling -∇φ from the anchor. Layered on top is a Hubble-flow body-force that gives every particle an additional outward radial acceleration a = H · r with H ≈ 0.05, where r is the displacement from the centre. Because the force is mass-independent, the same H acts on every particle. The critical radius where Hubble outward push balances central inward gravity is r_crit = √(Q/(2πH)) ≈ 12.6: probes at smaller r remain bound and orbit (with slightly reduced effective gravity), while probes outside r_crit accelerate outward and escape.

This fundamental misidentification of the interaction law and governing operator places the description in the lowest non-zero scoring band.
